========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, thanks, Doctor. I guess what brings me in is… I feel like I’ve got this motor humming under my skin whenever I’m supposed to sit still. Like, if I’m in a staff meeting at work, or even something like a church event briefing where everybody’s just seated and listening, I start feeling like I have to move.

I’ll shift around in the chair, tap my foot, stand up if I can get away with it, or I’ll find some excuse to go check something, get water, move a chair, whatever. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or not pay attention. It just feels really uncomfortable to stay still, like my body is pushing me to get up and do something. That’s the main thing that made me think I should talk to somebody.

That’s the main thing that made me think I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any new medications recently, such as antipsychotics, anti-nausea drugs, or other prescriptions, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think through it because I know sometimes you don’t connect those things unless somebody asks. But no, I haven’t started anything new like that that I can think of, and I don’t remember changing a dose or switching prescriptions right around when this got noticeable.\n\nI’m not on, you know, anything like what you mentioned—at least not that I’d recognize as an antipsychotic or nausea medicine. If you want, I can double-check the names of anything I take, but off the top of my head, I don’t think there was some new medication that lined up with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, it definitely goes way back. I mean, I don’t remember every detail, obviously, but I can remember being in grade school and getting in trouble because I’d pop up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Like the teacher would still be talking, and I’d already be halfway standing or blurting something out.

And I used to talk over the teacher sometimes—not trying to be disrespectful, but I’d just jump in before she was done. Or I’d rush ahead on assignments before all the directions were finished, and then I’d find out I did the wrong thing because I hadn’t waited. That happened enough that I remember it being a “me” thing, if that makes sense.

My mother used to say I was wound too tight even when I was a kid. That was her phrase. So… yeah, this isn’t something that just suddenly started in adulthood. It’s more like it’s always been there, and maybe I’ve just noticed it more in situations where I’m expected to sit still and behave like a grown man.

I’m not sure I’d say that happens “often,” at least not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. I mean, sometimes if somebody is talking for a long time, sure, my mind can drift a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than anybody else.

When I’m trying hard to pay attention, I usually feel more aware of my body wanting to move around than of my mind wandering, if that makes sense. Like I might be listening, but I’m shifting, or wanting to get up, or feeling impatient. But as far as losing the thread of a conversation or a task because my brain just goes somewhere else… I can’t really give you a strong yes on that. I’d have to think about it more, because it’s not the thing that stands out to me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that one I do notice. Not so much in a dramatic way like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll jump in before someone is finished talking.

At work, for example, if my supervisor starts giving me instructions for something in the building, I’ll sometimes cut in because I think I already know what he’s about to say. Like he’ll be halfway through explaining what needs to be cleaned or checked, and I’m already saying, “Okay, yeah, you want me to do this and that,” and then it turns out I got ahead of him and missed the actual point. Then he has to stop me and correct me.

It happens with church staff too. If they’re asking me to set something up or move things around, I’ll kind of answer before they finish laying it out. In my head it feels like I’m saving time, or I’m just already there before they are, but then sometimes I’m wrong. And then I feel embarrassed because I know I should’ve just waited two more seconds and let them finish the sentence.

It’s more like it’s always been there, and maybe I’ve just noticed it more in situations where I’m expected to sit still and behave like a grown man.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, do you also notice problems like losing focus during tasks, forgetting appointments or items, or having trouble finishing things you start?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one, honestly. I mean, I know everybody forgets things or gets distracted sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound like more than it is. When you say “losing focus,” do you mean like zoning out even when I’m trying to pay attention, or more like not being able to stay with a project?\n\nI guess what I can say is the sitting-still part is the thing I notice most, the thing that feels hardest to manage. With forgetting appointments or losing items, I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern, not the way I notice the restlessness. Same with finishing things—I’d have to think about that more. Nothing is jumping out at me as the main problem the way the needing-to-move feeling does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering or losing focus in the middle of conversations or tasks, even when you're trying hard to pay attention?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens “often,” at least not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. I mean, sometimes if somebody is talking for a long time, sure, my mind can drift a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than anybody else.\n\nWhen I’m trying hard to pay attention, I usually feel more aware of my body wanting to move around than of my mind wandering, if that makes sense. Like I might be listening, but I’m shifting, or wanting to get up, or feeling impatient. But as far as losing the thread of a conversation or a task because my brain just goes somewhere else… I can’t really give you a strong yes on that. I’d have to think about it more, because it’s not the thing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: In your day-to-day life now, do you often find yourself interrupting people mid-sentence, blurting out answers before a question is finished, or having trouble waiting your turn in lines or conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that one I do notice. Not so much in a dramatic way like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll jump in before someone is finished talking.\n\nAt work, for example, if my supervisor starts giving me instructions for something in the building, I’ll sometimes cut in because I think I already know what he’s about to say. Like he’ll be halfway through explaining what needs to be cleaned or checked, and I’m already saying, “Okay, yeah, you want me to do this and that,” and then it turns out I got ahead of him and missed the actual point. Then he has to stop me and correct me.\n\nIt happens with church staff too. If they’re asking me to set something up or move things around, I’ll kind of answer before they finish laying it out. In my head it feels like I’m saving time, or I’m just already there before they are, but then sometimes I’m wrong. And then I feel embarrassed because I know I should’ve just waited two more seconds and let them finish the sentence.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s been like one big blowup or anything, but yes, it has started causing real problems at work.

The main thing is with instructions. My supervisor will be telling me what needs to happen with a room turnover or what supplies to use or not use, and I’ll jump in too fast because I think I already understand where he’s going. Then I miss some piece of what he was actually saying. So later it looks like I didn’t listen, or like I’m arguing, when in my head I was trying to keep things moving.

It’s created friction, definitely. Like with room setups, cleaning decisions, supplies—those situations where there are a lot of little details and people want it done a certain way. I’ll move ahead or start responding before everything is clear, and then I get corrected. And it’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be difficult, but I can see how it comes across that way.

My supervisor has complained about it and corrected me more than once. It got to the point where he pushed me toward the employee assistance program, like, “You need to talk to somebody about this,” basically. That was kind of a wake-up call for me, because I can brush off one correction, but when it starts affecting how people see me at work, then… yeah, that’s a problem.

I mean, sometimes if somebody is talking for a long time, sure, my mind can drift a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than anybody else.\n\nWhen I’m trying hard to pay attention, I usually feel more aware of my body wanting to move around than of my mind wandering, if that makes sense. Like I might be listening, but I’m shifting, or wanting to get up, or feeling impatient. But as far as losing the thread of a conversation or a task because my brain just goes somewhere else… I can’t really give you a strong yes on that. I’d have to think about it more, because it’s not the thing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: In your day-to-day life now, do you often find yourself interrupting people mid-sentence, blurting out answers before a question is finished, or having trouble waiting your turn in lines or conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that one I do notice. Not so much in a dramatic way like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll jump in before someone is finished talking.\n\nAt work, for example, if my supervisor starts giving me instructions for something in the building, I’ll sometimes cut in because I think I already know what he’s about to say. Like he’ll be halfway through explaining what needs to be cleaned or checked, and I’m already saying, “Okay, yeah, you want me to do this and that,” and then it turns out I got ahead of him and missed the actual point. Then he has to stop me and correct me.\n\nIt happens with church staff too. If they’re asking me to set something up or move things around, I’ll kind of answer before they finish laying it out. In my head it feels like I’m saving time, or I’m just already there before they are, but then sometimes I’m wrong. And then I feel embarrassed because I know I should’ve just waited two more seconds and let them finish the sentence.\n\nDoctor: Have these restlessness and impulsivity issues caused real problems for you—like conflicts with your supervisor, being written up at work, or friction with people at church—because of jumping in or moving around when you shouldn't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s been like one big blowup or anything, but yes, it has started causing real problems at work.\n\nThe main thing is with instructions. My supervisor will be telling me what needs to happen with a room turnover or what supplies to use or not use, and I’ll jump in too fast because I think I already understand where he’s going. Then I miss some piece of what he was actually saying. So later it looks like I didn’t listen, or like I’m arguing, when in my head I was trying to keep things moving.\n\nIt’s created friction, definitely. Like with room setups, cleaning decisions, supplies—those situations where there are a lot of little details and people want it done a certain way. I’ll move ahead or start responding before everything is clear, and then I get corrected. And it’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be difficult, but I can see how it comes across that way.\n\nMy supervisor has complained about it and corrected me more than once. It got to the point where he pushed me toward the employee assistance program, like, “You need to talk to somebody about this,” basically. That was kind of a wake-up call for me, because I can brush off one correction, but when it starts affecting how people see me at work, then… yeah, that’s a problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think it does show up outside of work too, maybe more as the restlessness than the interrupting part.

At home, after a shift, I’ll tell myself I’m going to sit down for a while, but then I don’t really sit. I’ll go out to the garage and start puttering around, like moving tools from one spot to another, re-sorting a drawer, checking something that probably didn’t need checking right then. It’s not always some big project. Sometimes it’s just busy activity, like I’m still in work mode and my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m allowed to stop.

And at work it’s very much like that too. On my shifts, I’m kind of constantly moving from one thing to the next—mopping, then checking trash, then straightening chairs, then noticing something else and going over there. I don’t always pause between things. Some of that is just the nature of the job, obviously, because there’s always something to clean or fix up, but I can tell there’s also this feeling in me like I need to keep going.

So yes, outside work and church, I’d say the restlessness is there. The jumping in with people can happen some too, but the thing I notice most at home is more that I don’t settle down easily. Even when nothing is urgent, I find something to fiddle with.

Like, if I’m in a staff meeting at work, or even something like a church event briefing where everybody’s just seated and listening, I start feeling like I have to move.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, tap my foot, stand up if I can get away with it, or I’ll find some excuse to go check something, get water, move a chair, whatever. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or not pay attention. It just feels really uncomfortable to stay still, like my body is pushing me to get up and do something. That’s the main thing that made me think I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any new medications recently, such as antipsychotics, anti-nausea drugs, or other prescriptions, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think through it because I know sometimes you don’t connect those things unless somebody asks. But no, I haven’t started anything new like that that I can think of, and I don’t remember changing a dose or switching prescriptions right around when this got noticeable.\n\nI’m not on, you know, anything like what you mentioned—at least not that I’d recognize as an antipsychotic or nausea medicine. If you want, I can double-check the names of anything I take, but off the top of my head, I don’t think there was some new medication that lined up with it.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice this kind of restlessness or difficulty sitting still as far back as childhood or your teenage years, or is this something that started more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely goes way back. I mean, I don’t remember every detail, obviously, but I can remember being in grade school and getting in trouble because I’d pop up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Like the teacher would still be talking, and I’d already be halfway standing or blurting something out.\n\nAnd I used to talk over the teacher sometimes—not trying to be disrespectful, but I’d just jump in before she was done. Or I’d rush ahead on assignments before all the directions were finished, and then I’d find out I did the wrong thing because I hadn’t waited. That happened enough that I remember it being a “me” thing, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mother used to say I was wound too tight even when I was a kid. That was her phrase. So… yeah, this isn’t something that just suddenly started in adulthood. It’s more like it’s always been there, and maybe I’ve just noticed it more in situations where I’m expected to sit still and behave like a grown man.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, do you also notice problems like losing focus during tasks, forgetting appointments or items, or having trouble finishing things you start?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one, honestly. I mean, I know everybody forgets things or gets distracted sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound like more than it is. When you say “losing focus,” do you mean like zoning out even when I’m trying to pay attention, or more like not being able to stay with a project?\n\nI guess what I can say is the sitting-still part is the thing I notice most, the thing that feels hardest to manage. With forgetting appointments or losing items, I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern, not the way I notice the restlessness. Same with finishing things—I’d have to think about that more. Nothing is jumping out at me as the main problem the way the needing-to-move feeling does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering or losing focus in the middle of conversations or tasks, even when you're trying hard to pay attention?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens “often,” at least not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. I mean, sometimes if somebody is talking for a long time, sure, my mind can drift a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than anybody else.\n\nWhen I’m trying hard to pay attention, I usually feel more aware of my body wanting to move around than of my mind wandering, if that makes sense. Like I might be listening, but I’m shifting, or wanting to get up, or feeling impatient. But as far as losing the thread of a conversation or a task because my brain just goes somewhere else… I can’t really give you a strong yes on that. I’d have to think about it more, because it’s not the thing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: In your day-to-day life now, do you often find yourself interrupting people mid-sentence, blurting out answers before a question is finished, or having trouble waiting your turn in lines or conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that one I do notice. Not so much in a dramatic way like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll jump in before someone is finished talking.\n\nAt work, for example, if my supervisor starts giving me instructions for something in the building, I’ll sometimes cut in because I think I already know what he’s about to say. Like he’ll be halfway through explaining what needs to be cleaned or checked, and I’m already saying, “Okay, yeah, you want me to do this and that,” and then it turns out I got ahead of him and missed the actual point. Then he has to stop me and correct me.\n\nIt happens with church staff too. If they’re asking me to set something up or move things around, I’ll kind of answer before they finish laying it out. In my head it feels like I’m saving time, or I’m just already there before they are, but then sometimes I’m wrong. And then I feel embarrassed because I know I should’ve just waited two more seconds and let them finish the sentence.\n\nDoctor: Have these restlessness and impulsivity issues caused real problems for you—like conflicts with your supervisor, being written up at work, or friction with people at church—because of jumping in or moving around when you shouldn't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s been like one big blowup or anything, but yes, it has started causing real problems at work.\n\nThe main thing is with instructions. My supervisor will be telling me what needs to happen with a room turnover or what supplies to use or not use, and I’ll jump in too fast because I think I already understand where he’s going. Then I miss some piece of what he was actually saying. So later it looks like I didn’t listen, or like I’m arguing, when in my head I was trying to keep things moving.\n\nIt’s created friction, definitely. Like with room setups, cleaning decisions, supplies—those situations where there are a lot of little details and people want it done a certain way. I’ll move ahead or start responding before everything is clear, and then I get corrected. And it’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be difficult, but I can see how it comes across that way.\n\nMy supervisor has complained about it and corrected me more than once. It got to the point where he pushed me toward the employee assistance program, like, “You need to talk to somebody about this,” basically. That was kind of a wake-up call for me, because I can brush off one correction, but when it starts affecting how people see me at work, then… yeah, that’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same restlessness or jumping in before people finish talking at home with family or in other settings outside work and church?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does show up outside of work too, maybe more as the restlessness than the interrupting part.\n\nAt home, after a shift, I’ll tell myself I’m going to sit down for a while, but then I don’t really sit. I’ll go out to the garage and start puttering around, like moving tools from one spot to another, re-sorting a drawer, checking something that probably didn’t need checking right then. It’s not always some big project. Sometimes it’s just busy activity, like I’m still in work mode and my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m allowed to stop.\n\nAnd at work it’s very much like that too. On my shifts, I’m kind of constantly moving from one thing to the next—mopping, then checking trash, then straightening chairs, then noticing something else and going over there. I don’t always pause between things. Some of that is just the nature of the job, obviously, because there’s always something to clean or fix up, but I can tell there’s also this feeling in me like I need to keep going.\n\nSo yes, outside work and church, I’d say the restlessness is there. The jumping in with people can happen some too, but the thing I notice most at home is more that I don’t settle down easily. Even when nothing is urgent, I find something to fiddle with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0"], "note": "Patient endorses cross-situational hyperactivity-impulsivity (restlessness, interrupting, blurting answers) with childhood onset and functional impairment at work/church/home, meeting criteria for predominantly hyperactive-impulsive presentation. Inattentive symptoms are not clearly endorsed, but this does not rule out the hyperactive-impulsive subtype. No evidence of medication-induced akathisia given no new drug exposure. Candidates remain unchanged.", "is_final": false}

And it’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to be difficult, but I can see how it comes across that way.\n\nMy supervisor has complained about it and corrected me more than once. It got to the point where he pushed me toward the employee assistance program, like, “You need to talk to somebody about this,” basically. That was kind of a wake-up call for me, because I can brush off one correction, but when it starts affecting how people see me at work, then… yeah, that’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this same restlessness or jumping in before people finish talking at home with family or in other settings outside work and church?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does show up outside of work too, maybe more as the restlessness than the interrupting part.\n\nAt home, after a shift, I’ll tell myself I’m going to sit down for a while, but then I don’t really sit. I’ll go out to the garage and start puttering around, like moving tools from one spot to another, re-sorting a drawer, checking something that probably didn’t need checking right then. It’s not always some big project. Sometimes it’s just busy activity, like I’m still in work mode and my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m allowed to stop.\n\nAnd at work it’s very much like that too. On my shifts, I’m kind of constantly moving from one thing to the next—mopping, then checking trash, then straightening chairs, then noticing something else and going over there. I don’t always pause between things. Some of that is just the nature of the job, obviously, because there’s always something to clean or fix up, but I can tell there’s also this feeling in me like I need to keep going.\n\nSo yes, outside work and church, I’d say the restlessness is there. The jumping in with people can happen some too, but the thing I notice most at home is more that I don’t settle down easily. Even when nothing is urgent, I find something to fiddle with.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from the physical restlessness, do you also experience frequent worry, nervousness, or a racing mind that makes it hard to relax, even when you're not in a specific stressful situation?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 56 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think of it as worry, exactly. When I’m moving around or not settling, it feels more like my body is still geared up, if that makes sense, not necessarily like I’m sitting there thinking, “Oh no, what’s going to happen?”

I guess I’d have to pay closer attention to that, because I don’t usually label it as nervousness. If there’s something specific going on, sure, I can get concerned like anybody, but when there isn’t some particular stressful thing, I’m not sure I’d say I’m worrying all the time. It’s more that I have trouble just being still.

The “racing mind” part… I don’t know. Sometimes my thoughts move from one thing to the next, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by racing. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. The part I notice clearly is the not relaxing physically, more than feeling scared or anxious.

I guess what brings me in is… I feel like I’ve got this motor humming under my skin whenever I’m supposed to sit still. Like, if I’m in a staff meeting at work, or even something like a church event briefing where everybody’s just seated and listening, I start feeling like I have to move.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, tap my foot, stand up if I can get away with it, or I’ll find some excuse to go check something, get water, move a chair, whatever. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude or not pay attention. It just feels really uncomfortable to stay still, like my body is pushing me to get up and do something. That’s the main thing that made me think I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any new medications recently, such as antipsychotics, anti-nausea drugs, or other prescriptions, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I’m trying to think through it because I know sometimes you don’t connect those things unless somebody asks. If you want, I can double-check the names of anything I take, but off the top of my head, I don’t think there was some new medication that lined up with it.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice this kind of restlessness or difficulty sitting still as far back as childhood or your teenage years, or is this something that started more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely goes way back. I mean, I don’t remember every detail, obviously, but I can remember being in grade school and getting in trouble because I’d pop up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Like the teacher would still be talking, and I’d already be halfway standing or blurting something out.\n\nAnd I used to talk over the teacher sometimes—not trying to be disrespectful, but I’d just jump in before she was done. That happened enough that I remember it being a “me” thing, if that makes sense.\n\nMy mother used to say I was wound too tight even when I was a kid. That was her phrase. So… yeah, this isn’t something that just suddenly started in adulthood. It’s more like it’s always been there, and maybe I’ve just noticed it more in situations where I’m expected to sit still and behave like a grown man.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, do you also notice problems like losing focus during tasks, forgetting appointments or items, or having trouble finishing things you start?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one, honestly. I mean, I know everybody forgets things or gets distracted sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound like more than it is. When you say “losing focus,” do you mean like zoning out even when I’m trying to pay attention, or more like not being able to stay with a project?\n\nI guess what I can say is the sitting-still part is the thing I notice most, the thing that feels hardest to manage. With forgetting appointments or losing items, I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern, not the way I notice the restlessness. Same with finishing things—I’d have to think about that more. Nothing is jumping out at me as the main problem the way the needing-to-move feeling does.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering or losing focus in the middle of conversations or tasks, even when you're trying hard to pay attention?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that happens “often,” at least not in a way I’ve clearly noticed. I mean, sometimes if somebody is talking for a long time, sure, my mind can drift a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than anybody else.\n\nWhen I’m trying hard to pay attention, I usually feel more aware of my body wanting to move around than of my mind wandering, if that makes sense. Like I might be listening, but I’m shifting, or wanting to get up, or feeling impatient. But as far as losing the thread of a conversation or a task because my brain just goes somewhere else… I can’t really give you a strong yes on that. I’d have to think about it more, because it’s not the thing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: In your day-to-day life now, do you often find yourself interrupting people mid-sentence, blurting out answers before a question is finished, or having trouble waiting your turn in lines or conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that one I do notice. Not so much in a dramatic way like I’m trying to be rude, but I’ll jump in before someone is finished talking.\n\nAt work, for example, if my supervisor starts giving me instructions for something in the building, I’ll sometimes cut in because I think I already know what he’s about to say. Like he’ll be halfway through explaining what needs to be cleaned or checked, and I’m already saying, “Okay, yeah, you want me to do this and that,” and then it turns out I got ahead of him and missed the actual point. Then he has to stop me and correct me.\n\nIt happens with church staff too. If they’re asking me to set something up or move things around, I’ll kind of answer before they finish laying it out. In my head it feels like I’m saving time, or I’m just already there before they are, but then sometimes I’m wrong. And then I feel embarrassed because I know I should’ve just waited two more seconds and let them finish the sentence.\n\nDoctor: Have these restlessness and impulsivity issues caused real problems for you—like conflicts with your supervisor, being written up at work, or friction with people at church—because of jumping in or moving around when you shouldn't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s been like one big blowup or anything, but yes, it has started causing real problems at work.\n\nThe main thing is with instructions. My supervisor will be telling me what needs to happen with a room turnover or what supplies to use or not use, and I’ll jump in too fast because I think I already understand where he’s going. Then I miss some piece of what he was actually saying. So later it looks like I didn’t listen, or like I’m arguing, when in my head I was trying to keep things moving.\n\nIt’s created friction, definitely. Like with room setups, cleaning decisions, supplies—those situations where there are a lot of little details and people want it done a certain way. I’ll move ahead or start responding before everything is clear, and then I get corrected.       "Medication-induced akathisia ruled out (no new/changed psychotropic or antiemetic medications)",
      "Generalized anxiety ruled out (restlessness described as physical/motor, not accompanied by persistent worry or racing anxious cognition)",
      "Clear functional impairment demonstrated by supervisor complaints, repeated correction, and EAP referral"
    ]
  }
}

Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Like, if I’m in a staff meeting at work, or even something like a church event briefing where everybody’s just seated and listening, I start feeling like I have to move.\n\nI’ll shift around in the chair, tap my foot, stand up if I can get away with it, or I’ll find some excuse to go check something, get water, move a chair, whatever. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not really sure how to answer that one, honestly. I mean, I know everybody forgets things or gets distracted sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound like more than it is. When you say “losing focus,” do you mean like zoning out even when I’m trying to pay attention, or more like not being able to stay with a project?\n\nI guess what I can say is the sitting-still part is the thing I notice most, the thing that feels hardest to manage. With forgetting appointments or losing items, I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern, not the way I notice the restlessness. Same with finishing things—I’d have to think about that more. Nothing is jumping out at me as the main problem the way the needing-to-move feeling does.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

It’s not always some big project. Sometimes it’s just busy activity, like I’m still in work mode and my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m allowed to stop.\n\nAnd at work it’s very much like that too. On my shifts, I’m kind of constantly moving from one thing to the next—mopping, then checking trash, then straightening chairs, then noticing something else and going over there. I don’t always pause between things. Some of that is just the nature of the job, obviously, because there’s always something to clean or fix up, but I can tell there’s also this feeling in me like I need to keep going.\n\nSo yes, outside work and church, I’d say the restlessness is there. The jumping in with people can happen some too, but the thing I notice most at home is more that I don’t settle down easily.
